\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0
.
For all
s\in[m_{k}]
we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\geq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+(t-T_{3})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\geq t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert/2,

(39)

where the first step is by Lemma
E.23
and
\hat{T}_{4}^{-}<t^{*}
,
the second step is by the lower bound in Eq. (
24
) at
t=T_{3}
.
For all
s\in[m_{k}]
we also have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\leq tn\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

where the second step is by
\mathcal{A}(t)
.
By the definition of
T_{4}^{-}
, at
t=\hat{T}_{4}^{-}
we have

\displaystyle tn\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert/4

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}n\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1\pm o(1)}{1+\exp(\frac{2}{\pi}m_{k}t^{2}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2})}\cdot tn\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1\pm o(1)}{\exp(\frac{2}{\pi}m_{k}t^{2}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2})}\cdot tn\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

which implies that

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{4}^{-}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{4\sqrt{2}\sigma_{p}s}{\sqrt{\pi}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert}(1+o(1))\right)}\eta^{-1}m_{k}^{-1/2}\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1},

which gives a contradiction.
∎

Remark E.26
.

The upper bound for
T_{4}^{-}
also proves that
t^{*}=T_{4}^{-}
, which gives that for all
t\in[T_{3},T_{4}^{-}]
,
s\in[m_{k}]
, and
i,k\in[n]

\displaystyle\frac{s_{i,21}^{(t)}}{s_{k,21}^{(t)}}=1\pm o(1),

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert.

Moreover, for neuron
s
, suppose
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{3})}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle>0
, then at
t=T_{4}^{-}
, for all
s\in[m_{k}]
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=(t-T_{3})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{3})}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1\pm o(1)),

(40)

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=-(t-T_{3})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{3})}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=-t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1\pm o(1)),

(41)

where for query signal the first step is by Lemma
E.23

and the second step is by the bound Eq.
24
.
The proof of statement for key signal is similar.

E.7.2
Stage IV.b

This stage studies the dynamics in the transition interval
[T_{4}^{-},T_{4}^{+}]
.
For
T_{4}^{-}\leq t\leq T_{4}^{+}
,
given neuron
s
, for those samples where query/key noise align with the query/key signal, query/key noise dynamics keep unchanged, which will be defined later.
On the other hands, for those samples where query/key noise do not align with query/key signal, query noise dynamics keep unchanged.
However, key noise start to decrease but are still greater than zero at
T_{4}^{+}
.

Let
T_{4}^{+}
be the first time the following condition holds

\displaystyle s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|,

(42)

for all
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]
. Note that at
T_{4}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}(1+o(1))ns_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|<\frac{1}{2}ns_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|,

which implies
T_{4}^{+}\geq T_{4}^{-}
,
T_{4}^{+}
is well-defined.

Let

\displaystyle E_{i}^{(T_{3})}:=\left\{s\in[m_{k}]:\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right\},

\displaystyle E_{s}^{(T_{3})}:=\left\{i\in[n]:\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right\}.

all neurons with
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}
are called aligned neurons.
The next lemma studies the concentration behaviour about
\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|
. This lemma is crucial to upper bound
s_{i,21}^{(t)}
for
T_{4}^{-}\leq t\leq T_{4}^{+}
, and further upper bound
T_{4}^{+}
.

Lemma E.27
.

Suppose that
\delta>0

Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|\geq m_{k}/2-\tilde{O}(m_{k}n^{-1/2}+m_{k}^{1/2})\geq m_{k}/2-\tilde{O}(\sqrt{m_{k}}),

for all
i\in[n]
.

Proof.

We can write
E_{i}^{(T_{3})}
as

\displaystyle E_{i}^{(T_{3})}=\left\{s\in[m_{k}]:\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right\},

where this is by the sign of
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle
does not change for
[T_{2},T_{3}]
and the sign of
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
is the same as that of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle
, i.e., Lemma
E.9
and
E.11
.
Then we have

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{m_{k}}\mathbb{E}[\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0]+\mathbb{P}[\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle<0,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle<0]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[X_{s,i}>0,\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{s,i}>0]+\mathbb{P}[X_{s,i}<0,\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{s,i}<0]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}-O(n^{-1/2}),

where the third step by that the sign of
X_{s,i}
determines the sign of
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{2})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle
,
the last step is by Lemma
E.6
.
Then, by Hoeffding’s inequality, with probability at least
1-\delta/n

\displaystyle\left|\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|-pm_{k}\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{m_{k}}{2}\log(2n/\delta)}.

Finally, by the condition
n=\Omega(m_{k}^{2})
, we get the conclusion.
∎

We do not directly characterize the dynamics for
[T_{4}^{-},T_{4}^{+}]
.
Instead, we characterize for
[T_{4}^{-}],(1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}
, where
\theta
is a large constant in
(0,1)
, and show that the definition of
T_{4}^{+}
are satisfied, which characterize he dynamics before
T_{4}^{+}
and give an upper bound for
T_{4}^{+}
simultaneously.
Specifically, we use induction on
t
to simultaneously prove the following properties for all
t=T_{4}^{-},\dots,(1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}
:

•

\mathcal{A}(t)
, the monotonicity of query noise aligned with signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}.

•

\mathcal{B}(t)
, the monotonicity of key noise aligned with signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}.

•

\mathcal{C}(t)
, the monotonicity of query noise unaligned with signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}.

•

\mathcal{D}(t)
, the sign does not change for unaligned key noise: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle.

•

\mathcal{E}(t)
, the monotonicity of query signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert.

•

\mathcal{F}(t)
, the sign does not change for key signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle.

•

\mathcal{G}(t)
, the linear-with-
t
estimation: for all
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=-\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1\pm o(1)).

Remark E.28
.

Before stating the results in this section, we first recall and summarize the results until
T_{4}^{-}
.
WLOG, given neuron
s
, we suppose that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{2})}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0
in this section below.
Then we have
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{3})}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle>0
and
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{3})}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle<0
.
Then at
t=T_{4}^{-}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=-t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=-t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1\pm o(1)),

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=-t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1\pm o(1)).

This gives that
\mathcal{A}(T_{4}^{-})
,

\mathcal{B}(T_{4}^{-})
,
\mathcal{C}(T_{4}^{-})
,
\mathcal{D}(T_{4}^{-})
,
\mathcal{E}(T_{4}^{-})
,
\mathcal{F}(T_{4}^{-})
hold.

To prove these properties, we need some further properties after
T_{4}^{-}
.

Lemma E.29

(Properties after
T_{4}^{-}
)
.

Let
\theta
be a large constant in
(0,1)
.
For all
t\geq T_{4}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle(2T_{4}^{-}-t)\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1-\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))\leq\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\leq t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

\displaystyle(2T_{4}^{-}-t)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1-o(1))\leq\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\leq t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1+o(1)),

and for all
T_{4}^{-}\leq t\leq(1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle.

Particularly, For all
0\leq\theta^{\prime}\leq\theta
,
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]
,
setting
t=(1+\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1-\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))\leq\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{((1+\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\leq(1+\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

\displaystyle(1-\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-}\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1-o(1))\leq\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{((1+\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|\leq(1+\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-}\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1+o(1)).

Proof of Lemma
E.29
.

Suppose
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle>0
, then we have for all
t\geq T_{4}^{-}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\leq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+(t-T_{4}^{-})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}\leq t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

where the first step is by that the magnitude of single update of query/key noise is always
\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}
,
the second step is by summary above and Lemma
C.4
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\geq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-(t-T_{4}^{-})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}\geq(2T_{4}^{-}-t)\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})).

Set
t=(1+\theta^{\prime})T_{4}^{-}
gives the conclusion.
Also, the lower bound gives

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq(2T_{4}^{-}-t)\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle\geq(1-\theta)T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))>0,

where the last step is by
\theta
is a constant.
Similarly, we can prove the statement for
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
.
∎

Remark E.30
.

Note that Lemma
E.29
proves the
\mathcal{D}(t)
and
\mathcal{F}(t)
for all
T_{2}^{-}\leq t\leq(1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}
.

Claim E.31
.

\mathcal{G}(t),\mathcal{F}(t)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{A}(t)
.

Claim E.32
.

\mathcal{G}(t)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{B}(t)
.

Claim E.33
.

\mathcal{D}(t),\mathcal{F}(t)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(t)
.

Claim E.34
.

\mathcal{B}(t)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{E}(t)
.

Claim E.35
.

\mathcal{G}(t),\mathcal{A}(t),\mathcal{B}(t),\mathcal{C}(t),\mathcal{E}(t)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{G}(t+1)
.

Proof of Claim
E.31
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0,

where the first step is by Eq. (
10
),
the second step is by
\mathcal{F}(t)
,
the last step is by
\mathcal{G}(t)
.
∎

Proof of Claim
E.32
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0,

where the first step is by Eq. (
12
),
the second step is by
\mathcal{G}(t)
.
∎

Proof of Claim
E.33
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0,

where the first step is by Eq. (
10
),
the second step is by
\mathcal{F}(t)
,
the last step is by
\mathcal{D}(t)
.
∎

Proof of Claim
E.34
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}(-\ell_{i}^{\prime(t)}s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)})\cdot\left(-y_{i}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8\sqrt{\pi}}t\eta\sigma_{p}s(1\pm o(1))\cdot(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-n\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-n\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sum_{i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sum_{i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-n\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sum_{i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+(t-T_{4}^{-})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}+\sum_{i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-(t-T_{4}^{-})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-n\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sum_{i\in[n]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+(t-T_{4}^{-})\eta\sigma_{p}s/\sqrt{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-n\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sum_{i\in[n]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+(t-T_{4}^{-})\eta(\sigma_{p}s/\sqrt{2}-n\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert)>0,

where the first step is by Eq. (
11
),
the second step is by the concentration of
\ell_{i}^{\prime}
and
s_{i,11}^{(t)}
for
t\leq T_{4}
, i.e., Lemma
E.14
and
E.15
,
the fifth step is by
\mathcal{B}(t)
,
the sixth step is by the lower bound Eq. (
22
),
the last step is guaranteed by the dynamics in
[T_{3},T_{4}^{-}]
.
∎

Proof of Claim
E.35
.

This claim is straightforward.
∎

The following lemma gives an upper bound for
T_{4}^{+}
.

Lemma E.36
.

Let
\theta_{c}
be a small constant satisfying
0.99(1+\theta_{c})-O(\theta_{c}/\sqrt{m_{k}})>1
,
then we have

\displaystyle T_{4}^{+}\leq(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},

(43)

and for all
t\in[(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}]
we have

\displaystyle s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|.

Proof.

For fixed
\theta\in[\theta_{c},1-\theta_{c}]
, at
t=(1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}
, for all
i\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in[m_{k}]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sum_{s\in[m_{k}]/E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|\frac{2}{\pi}t^{2}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))+(m_{k}-\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|)\frac{2}{\pi}t(2T_{4}^{-}-t)\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|t^{2}+(m_{k}-\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|)t(2T_{4}^{-}-t))\cdot\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|(1+\theta)^{2}T_{4}^{-2}+(m_{k}-\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|)(1+\theta)(1-\theta)T_{4}^{-2}\cdot\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ ((\frac{m_{k}}{2}-o({m_{k}}))(1+\theta)^{2}+(\frac{m_{k}}{2}+o({m_{k}}))(1-\theta^{2})\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ ((1+\theta)m_{k}-(\theta^{2}+\theta)o(m_{k}))\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

(44)

where the second step is by
\mathcal{G}(t)
and Lemma
E.29
,
the fifth step is by Lemma
E.27
.

Then, we have

\displaystyle s_{i,21}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\left(\sum_{s\in[m_{k}]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right)}{\exp\left(\sum_{s\in[m_{k}]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right)+\exp\left(\sum_{s\in[m_{k}]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1\pm o(1)}{1\pm o(1)+\exp\left(((1+\theta)m_{k}-(\theta^{2}+\theta)O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm o(1)}{1\pm o(1)+\exp(((1+\theta)m_{k}-(\theta^{2}+\theta)O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2})\exp(\pm\tilde{O}(\frac{2}{\pi}m_{k}T_{4}^{-2}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{3/2}))}

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm o(1)}{1\pm o(1)+\exp(((1+\theta)m_{k}-(\theta^{2}+\theta)O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2})(1\pm o(1))}

\displaystyle=\frac{1\pm o(1)}{1+\exp(((1+\theta)m_{k}-(\theta^{2}+\theta)O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1\pm o(1)}{\exp(((1+\theta)m_{k}-(\theta^{2}+\theta)O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2})},

(45)

where the first step is by definition, the second step is by Eq. (
28
), (
27
), and (
44
), the fourth step is by
T_{4}^{-}\lesssim T_{4}
and
m_{k}T_{4}^{2}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2}=o(1)
.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\penalty\ s_{i,21}^{((1+\theta)T_{4}^{-})}s_{i,22}^{((1+\theta)T_{4}^{-})}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{((1+\theta)T_{4}^{-})}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1+o(1)}{\exp(((1+\theta)m_{k}-(\theta^{2}+\theta)O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))\cdot T_{4}^{-2}\frac{2}{\pi}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}^{2}s^{2})}\cdot 1\cdot(1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))\cdot\left(\frac{3\sqrt{2}n\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert}{\sigma_{p}s}\right)^{0.99(1+\theta)-O(\theta/\sqrt{m_{k}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{9}(1+\theta)T_{4}^{-}\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}s_{i,11}^{((1+\theta)T_{4}^{-})}s_{i,12}^{((1+\theta)T_{4}^{-})}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{((1+\theta)T_{4}^{-})}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|,

where the first step is by Eq. (
45
) and
\mathcal{G}(t)
,
the second step is by Lemma
E.21
,
the third step is by
0.99(1+\theta)-O(\theta/\sqrt{m_{k}})>1
,
the last step is by
\mathcal{G}(t)
and
the concentration results in Lemma
E.14
,
thus the conclusion holds.
∎

E.7.3
Stage IV.c

T_{4}
is a timestep where
s_{i,11}^{(t)}=1/2+o(1)
,
we characterize the dynamics in this interval.

WLOG, given neuron
s
, we suppose that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{2})}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0
in this section below.

Specifically, we use induction on
t
to simultaneously prove the following properties for all
t=(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},\dots,T_{4}
:

•

\mathcal{A}(t)
, the monotonicity of query noise aligned with signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}.

•

\mathcal{B}(t)
, the monotonicity of key noise aligned with signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}.

•

\mathcal{C}(t)
, the fate of query noise unaligned with signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}
,
for
t\leq(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{+})}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1},

and for
t\geq(2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{+})}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}.

•

\mathcal{D}(t)
, the monotonicity of key noise unaligned with signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}.

•

\mathcal{E}(t)
, the monotonicity of query signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert.

•

\mathcal{F}(t)
: the monotonicity of key signal: for any
s\in[m_{k}]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert.

•

\mathcal{G}(t)
, the linear-with-
t
estimation: for all
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)t\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),\forall i\in E_{s}^{(T_{3})}

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)t\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert(1\pm o(1)).

•

\mathcal{H}(t)
: for any
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]

\displaystyle s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|.

Remark E.37
.

By Lemma
E.36
, we have
\mathcal{H}(t)
holds for
t\in[(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}]
.
The
\mathcal{A}(t)
,
\mathcal{B}(t)
,
\mathcal{E}(t)
and
\mathcal{G}(t)
for
t\in[(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}]
are already shown in the induction statement in the last section. Actually, the proof of
\mathcal{A}(t)
,
\mathcal{B}(t)
,
\mathcal{E}(t)
and
\mathcal{G}(t)
for all subsequent
t
are same as those in section
E.7.2
, thus we omit them in this section.

Claim E.38
.

\mathcal{H}(t)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(t),\mathcal{F}(t)
.

Proof of Claim
E.38
.

We have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\penalty\ s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle,

where the first step is by Eq. (
12
),
the second step is by
\mathcal{H}(t)
and

\displaystyle\penalty\ \langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i\in[n]}(-l_{i}^{\prime(t)})\cdot\left(-s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle-s_{i,21}^{(t)}s_{i,22}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i\in[n]}(-l_{i}^{\prime(t)})\cdot(-s_{i,11}^{(t)}s_{i,12}^{(t)}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle,

where the first step is by Eq. (
13
),
the second step is by
\mathcal{H}(t)
.
∎

The next lemma gives an one side bound for unaligned key noise for
t\geq(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
.

Lemma E.39
.

Let
t\geq(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
, suppose
\mathcal{D}(t)
holds for
t=(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},\dots,\tilde{t}
,
then for all
s\in[m_{k}]
,
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}
we have

\displaystyle\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle\leq(2(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}-t)\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})).

Proof.

Since
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{2})}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle>0
,
we have
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle<0
for
s\in[m_{k}]
,
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}
.
We can show

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle+(t-(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle+(t-(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}-\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=-T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1\pm\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))+(t-(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}-\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-})\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\geq-(2(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}-t)\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

where the first step is by
\mathcal{D}(t)
for
t\in[(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},\tilde{t}]
,
the second step is by Lemma
E.29
,
the third step is by the argument in Remark
E.28
,
the last step is by Lemma
C.4
.
∎

Now we only need to and are ready to show
\mathcal{H}(t)
holds for
t=(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}+1,\dots,T_{4}
.
Suppose
\mathcal{A}(t^{\prime}),\dots,\mathcal{H}(t^{\prime})
hold for
t^{\prime}\leq t
, we want to show
\mathcal{H}(t+1)
holds.
We split to two stages to prove where the first one is
[(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},(2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}]
. In this stage, we can bound the magnitude of unaligned key noise
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle
by Lemma
E.29
and
E.39
.
Note that

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1})(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1})-\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+2t\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1))),

(46)

where the last step is by
\mathcal{G}(t)
,
and

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in[n]/E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{t\geq T_{4,s,i}^{Q,\text{flip}}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sum_{t<T_{4,s,i}^{Q,\text{flip}}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{t<T_{4,s,i}^{Q,\text{flip}},\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle>0}-\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}-\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\sum_{t<T_{4,s,i}^{Q,\text{flip}},\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle<0}-\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}-\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\sum_{t\geq T_{4,s,i}^{Q,\text{flip}},\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle>0}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\sum_{t\geq T_{4,s,i}^{Q,\text{flip}},\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle<0}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}-\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in[n]/E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}-\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}-\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|).

Furthermore, when
(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}\leq t\leq(2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
, for
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}
, we have

\displaystyle-3\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))\leq\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(T_{4}^{-})}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle)\cdot\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle\leq 3\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-}\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2})),

(47)

where the upper bound is by Lemma
E.39
, and the lower bound is by Lemma
E.29
.
Therefore, for
(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}t\leq(2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
we have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in[n]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|)

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\sum_{s\in[n]/E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+2t\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))-(m_{k}-\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|)(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+(t+3\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (\frac{m_{k}}{2}-O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+2t\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))-(\frac{m_{k}}{2}+O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+(t+3\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ -O(\sqrt{m_{k}})\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+(\frac{m_{k}}{2}(t-3\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-})-O(\sqrt{m_{k}})(t+\theta_{c}T_{4}^{-}))\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}(1\pm o(1)))>0,

where the second step is by Eq. (
46
) for aligned part,
Lemma
E.29
for upper bound of unaligned query noise and
Eq. (
47
) for upper bound of unaligned key noise,
the third step is by concentration for
\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|
in Lemma
E.27
.
Combined with
\mathcal{H}(t^{\prime})
for
(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
, we have

\displaystyle s_{i,21}^{(t+1)}s_{i,22}^{(t+1)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq s_{i,21}^{((1+\theta)T_{4}^{-})}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{9}(1+t)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2}s_{i,11}^{(t+1)}s_{i,12}^{(t+1)}\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t+1)}_{Q,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right|,

where the first step is by induction, the second step is similar to the proof in Lemma
E.36
,
which implies
\mathcal{H}(t+1)
holds.

Note that now we have
\mathcal{D}(t^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{F}(t^{\prime})
hold for
t=(2-\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-},\dots,(2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
. Then
for
t\geq(2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
,
suppose
\mathcal{F}(t)
and
\mathcal{D}(t)
hold for
t^{\prime}\leq t
,
for all
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]/E_{s}^{(T_{3})}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)}-\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}^{(t)}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq(t-2(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-})\eta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sigma_{p}s(1+\tilde{O}(s^{-1/2}))>0,

where the first step is by Eq. (
10
),
the second step is by
\mathcal{F}(t)
,
the last step is by Lemma
E.39
.
Combined with
\mathcal{D}(t)
, when
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle<0
(note that we suppose
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T_{3})},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle>0
, in more general case this is
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
), this gives

\displaystyle\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|=\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|.

(48)

Note that at
t=(2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{((2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{((2+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-})},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|\leq(2+6\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)),

(49)

where the upper bound for query noise is by Lemma
E.29
and the upper bound for key noise is by Lemma
E.39
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in[n]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{s\in E_{i}^{(T_{3})}}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|)

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\sum_{s\in[n]/E_{i}^{(T_{3})},\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}(\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+2t\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))-(m_{k}-\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|)(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+(2+6\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ (\frac{m_{k}}{2}-O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+2t\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))-(\frac{m_{k}}{2}+O(\sqrt{m_{k}}))(\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+(2+6\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\eta^{2}(1\pm o(1)))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ -O(\sqrt{m_{k}})\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}+(m_{k}(t-(1+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-})-O(\sqrt{m_{k}})(t+(1+3\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}))\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}(1\pm o(1)))>0,

where the second step is by Eq. (
46
) for aligned part,
Eq. (
48
) and (
49
) for unaligned part,
the third step is by concentration for
\left|E_{i}^{(T_{3})}\right|
in Lemma
E.27
.
Combined with
\mathcal{H}(t)
, this implies
\mathcal{H}(t+1)
holds.
Then we have
\mathcal{H}(t)
holds for all
(1+\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}\leq t\leq T_{4}
.

Remark E.40
.

When
t=(4+7\theta_{c})T_{4}^{-}
, we have that for all
s\in[m_{k}]
and
i\in[n]

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle.

Finally, for all
s\in[m_{k}]
,
i\in[n]
and
t\geq T_{4}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1},

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\right\rVert_{1},

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t+1)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle)\eta\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert,

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle=_{\sgn}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=_{\sgn}-\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle.

E.8
Proof of Theorem
3.2

In this section, we aim to prove the Theorem
3.2
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

By Lemma
E.41
and
E.42
, we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma E.41

(Training and Logistic Test Loss)
.

For any fixed
\epsilon>0
,
let
T=2\log(\epsilon^{-1})\eta^{-1}\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1}
, we have
L_{S}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)})\leq\epsilon
and
L_{\mathcal{D}}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)})\geq 0.1
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+\eta T\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\pm\tilde{O}(\sigma_{0}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert m_{v}^{-1/2})+2\log(\epsilon^{-1})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Theta(\log(\epsilon^{-1})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1}),

where the last step is by
\tilde{O}(\sigma_{0}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert m_{v}^{-1/2})=o(\eta T\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert)
.
Then, for all
i\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle y_{i}f(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}_{i})

\displaystyle=(s_{i,11}^{(T)}+s_{i,21}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+(s_{i,12}^{(T)}+s_{i,22}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq(s_{i,12}^{(T)}+s_{i,22}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq 0.9\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\log(\epsilon^{-1}),

where the first step is by the definition of
f
,
the second step is by
s_{i,11}^{(T)}\geq 0
,
s_{i,21}^{(T)}\geq 0
and
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\geq 0
,
the third step is by
s_{i,12}^{(T)}\geq 0

s_{i,22}^{(T)}\geq 0.9
,
the last step is by Lemma
E.8
and the definition of
T
.
This implies
\ell_{i}^{(T)}=\log(1+\exp(-y_{i}f_{i}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{x}}_{i})))\leq\epsilon

and thus
L_{S}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)})\leq\epsilon
.

On the other hand, for a new data point
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{x}}=(y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}})^{\top}
, let event
\mathcal{E}
be the event that
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}
has disjoint support with
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{n}
. Then when
\mathcal{E}
holds, we have

\displaystyle yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=(s_{11}^{(T)}+s_{21}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+(s_{12}^{(T)}+s_{22}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 2\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+2\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{O}(\log(\epsilon^{-1})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1}+\sigma_{0}\sigma_{p}s^{1/2}m_{v}^{-1/2})

\displaystyle\leq 1,

where the second step is by
s_{11}^{(T)},s_{12}^{(T)},s_{21}^{(T)},s_{22}^{(T)}\leq 1
,
the last step is by
\sigma_{0}\sigma_{p}s^{1/2}=o(1)
.
And when
\mathcal{E}
does not hold, we have

\displaystyle yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=(s_{11}^{(T)}+s_{21}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+(s_{12}^{(T)}+s_{22}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 2\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+2\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq O(\log(\epsilon^{-1})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1}+\log(\epsilon^{-1}))

\displaystyle\leq O(\log(\epsilon^{-1})).

Note that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}]\geq 1-n^{-2}
, which gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\ell(yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}))]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(\mathcal{E})\ell(yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{x}}))]+\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(\mathcal{E}^{c})\ell(yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{x}}))]

\displaystyle\geq(1-n^{-2})\log(1+e^{-1})\geq 0.1,

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma E.42

(Attention layer all attends to noise patch)
.

Let

T_{\mathrm{attn}}=\Theta(\log(\sigma_{p}s/\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert)\eta^{-1}m_{k}^{-1/2}\sigma_{p}^{-1/2}s^{-1/2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert^{-1/2})
, we have

s_{i,11}^{(T_{\mathrm{attn}})}=o(1)
,
s_{i,21}^{(T_{\mathrm{attn}})}=o(1)
, for all
i\in[n]
.

Proof.

By the previous analysis, we already have
s_{i,21}^{(t)}=o(1)
for
t\geq T_{4}^{-}
.
For
t\geq T_{\mathrm{attn}}

\displaystyle s_{i,11}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp\left(\sum_{s\in[m_{k}]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right)}{\exp\left(\sum_{s\in[m_{k}]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\right)+\exp\left(\sum_{s\in[m_{k}]}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(t)},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\exp\left(2m_{k}t^{2}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert^{2}\right)}{\exp\left(2m_{k}t^{2}\eta^{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert^{2}\right)+\exp\left(m_{k}t^{2}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}s\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert/4\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\exp\left(\Omega(m_{k}t^{2}\eta^{2}\sigma_{p}s\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert)\right)}

\displaystyle\leq o(1),

where the first step is by definition,
the second step is by
\mathcal{H}(t)
in Stage IV.c, Remark.
E.40
, and the concentration in Lemma
E.27
,
the last step is by
t\geq T_{\mathrm{attn}}
.
∎

Appendix F
Discussion on Extensions

F.1
0-1 Test Loss

In this section, we talk about the magntiude of 0-1 test loss.
In binary classification,
constant logistic test loss doesn’t necessarily mean constant 0-1 test loss.
And actually, we find it depends on the network initialization parameter
\sigma_{0}
.

The 0-1 Test Loss is defined as

\displaystyle L_{\mathcal{D}}^{0-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}):=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})>0)]=\mathbb{P}[yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})>0].

The value of
yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})
depends on two components, the signal part
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
, which continuously increases during training, and the noise part
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle
, which is random for the unseen noise
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}
. With probability at least
1-n^{-2}
, the unseen
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}
is disjoint with all training data
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}
.

If
\sigma_{0}
is small enough, i.e.,

\sigma_{0}=o(\sigma_{p}^{-2}s^{-3/2}m_{v}^{1/2})
, then the noise part can be ignored:
By the disjoint property, we can use the estimate at initialization for unseen noise.
By Lemma
C.6
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\Theta(\log(\epsilon^{-1})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1})\gg O(m_{v}^{-1/2}\sigma_{0}\sigma_{p}s^{1/2})=|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle|.

This is similar for

\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
v.s.
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle
, and

\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
v.s.
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle
,
which gives
s_{11}^{(T)},s_{21}^{(T)}=\Omega(1)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=(s_{11}^{(T)}+s_{21}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+(s_{12}^{(T)}+s_{22}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\Theta(\log(\epsilon^{-1})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1})-\tilde{O}(m_{v}^{-1/2}\sigma_{0}\sigma_{p}s^{1/2})

\displaystyle>0,

which implies a small 0-1 loss

\mathbb{P}[yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})>0]\geq 1-n^{-O(1)}
.

On the other hand, if
\sigma_{0}
is large, i.e.,
\sigma_{0}=\Omega(\sigma_{p}^{-2}s^{-3/2}m_{v}^{1/2})
but still satisfies Condition 4.1, then
we have

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle=\Theta(\log(\epsilon^{-1})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\right\rVert\sigma_{p}^{-1}s^{-1})\ll\Omega(m_{v}^{-1/2}\sigma_{0}\sigma_{p}s^{1/2})=|\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle|.

This is similar for

\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
v.s.
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle
, and

\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
v.s.
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle
,
which gives
s_{11}^{(T)},s_{21}^{(T)}=1/2\pm o(1)
since the symmetry of unseen noise. This means that the softmax outputs are random. Then,

\displaystyle yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=(s_{11}^{(T)}+s_{21}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+(s_{12}^{(T)}+s_{22}^{(T)})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Omega(1)\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}}^{(T)},y\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}\rangle,

which implies
|\mathbb{P}[yf(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(T)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})>0]-1/2|\leq 1-n^{-O(1)}
.
Note that the condition regarding
\sigma_{0}
here is not included in our Condition 4.1.

F.2
Extension to Longer Contexts

In this section, we talk about how to extend our current theory to longer context lengths with additional assumptions.

We consider two case of extension:

Noise patches are the same within one sample.

Note that many works use similar settings, e.g.,
Tarzanagh et al. (2023a)
;
Vasudeva et al. (2024)
;
Sheen et al. (2024)
assume all non-optimal tokens are the same in some way.
If the data
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}
has
L
patches
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}=[\boldsymbol{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{x}}^{(L)}]
where the first
L/2
patches are signal vectors
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}
and the latter
L/2
patches are noise vectors
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}^{(l)}
, and all noise patches are the same, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}^{(l)}=\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}^{(l^{\prime})}
for
L/2<l,l^{\prime}\leq L
, then the training dynamics are the same as in the
L=2
setting in the paper. If we change the ratio of number of signal patches to noise patches, e.g., 1 signal patch and
L-1
noise patches, it remains essentially the same except that the signal/noise-signal softmax is around
1/L
while (the sum of) signal/noise-noise softmax is around
(L-1)/L
at initialization. Note that in this case the softmax of all noise patches have the same value (no single patch becomes dominant).

Noise patches are different within one sample.

We remark that WLOG we can consider
1

\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}
and
(L-1)

\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}
vectors. With high probability, the
n(L-1)
noise patches are disjoint. The main difficulty here is the
competition between noise patches
, which can be seen in the gradient and increase speed. To address or avoid this issue, one solution is to assume some form of sparsity in the dimension of context length. For example,
Tarzanagh et al. (2023a)
;
Vasudeva et al. (2024)
;
Sheen et al. (2024)
assume there is only one optimal token in one sample, which has a large gap from the non-optimal ones.

Jiang et al. (2024)
assumes the second noise vector
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{2}
is greater than remain noise
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{3},\dots,\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{n}
in one data point.
In our case, we need to assume for each sample there is a noise patch
L
(WLOG, we assume it is the last patch) such that
\sum_{l^{\prime}=2}^{L-1}\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}^{(l^{\prime})}\|_{1}=o(\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}^{(l^{\prime})}\|_{L})
. We can achieve this by applying large sparsity
s
and/or large std
\sigma_{p}
on this patch. Note that this large patch across different samples can be in different positions. We provide a detailed example here: First, we still have stage I since the gradient of value does not change. The gradient of query is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}}L_{S}({\bf W})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}^{\prime}y_{i}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\bf x}_{i}^{(l)}\sum_{a<b}s_{i,la}s_{i,lb}\langle\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}}_{V,j},{\bf x}_{i}^{(a)}-{\bf x}_{i}^{(b)}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s},{\bf x}_{i}^{(a)}-{\bf x}_{i}^{(b)}\rangle,

and the inner product is

\displaystyle\langle\sgn(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{Q,s}}L_{S}),y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}^{(l)}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}^{(l)}\|_{1}\sgn(\sum_{a<b}s_{i,la}s_{i,lb}\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}},{\bf x}_{i}^{(a)}-{\bf x}_{i}^{(b)}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s},{\bf x}_{i}^{(a)}-{\bf x}_{i}^{(b)}\rangle)

\displaystyle\approx-\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}^{(l)}\|_{1}\sgn(\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}^{(L)}-\sum_{a<L}{\bf x}_{i}^{(a)}\rangle).

We approximate the inner sum with only one term by 1) concentration of softmax to
1/L
, 2) the assumption
\sum_{l^{\prime}=2}^{L-1}\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}^{(l^{\prime})}\|_{1}=o(\|\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}^{(l^{\prime})}\|_{L})
, which implies the dominance of
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{v}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}^{(L)}\rangle
after stage I, and the dominance of
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{K,s}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}^{(L)}\rangle
. The approximation for the update of key noise is essentially the same, thus ensuring the alignment between the largest query and the largest key, thus extending our result for
L=2
. Subsequently, due to the gap in the
L_{1}
norm, the softmax at the position of the largest noise patch will converge to 1, while the others will go to zero.

F.3
Extension to Joint Training of Linear Layer

In this section, we talk about how to extend our main theoretical results, which fix the second linear layer, to the joint training of softmax attention layer and linear layer.

Firstly, we would like to remark that the value matrix in our model setting already acts as a linear layer while many works
Tarzanagh et al. (2023a)
;
Tarzanagh et al. (2023b)
on analyzing training dynamics of transformers fix the value matrix.
In our specific case, we can extend the current theory to the joint training of the softmax attention layer and linear layer.

We can write the gradient with respect to the parameters in the second layer as follows:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta_{j,r}}L_{S}(W)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\ell_{i}^{\prime}j[(s_{i,11}+s_{i,21})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{V,j,r},y_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle+(s_{i,12}+s_{i,22})\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{V,j,r},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle],

where
\theta_{j,r}
denotes the parameter for the second layer
F_{j}
at the
r
-th entry, initialized with
j/m_{v}
, for
1\leq r\leq m_{v}
. During stage I, it moves at most
T_{1}\eta
which is negligible compared to the initialization
1/m_{v}
, and will not affect our analysis for value in stage I. After stage I, the terms
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{V,j,r},jy_{i}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\xi}}_{i}\rangle
become positive for all
1\leq i\leq n
and larger than the value signal term
\langle\boldsymbol{\mathbf{w}}_{V,j,r},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{\mu}}\rangle
, which leads to that

\sgn(\nabla_{\theta_{j,r}}L_{S}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}))=-j
. Consequently, all
\theta_{1,r}
keep increasing while all
\theta_{-1,r}
keep decreasing.
Until then, all
\theta_{j,r}
’s sign are the same as initialization and will not change during the rest of training.
Therefore, it will not affect the analysis for other parameters.

In summary,
the key to the joint training extension lies in the different magnitudes of initialization between the second layer weights and the value matrix, which is
1/m_{v}
and
\sigma_{0}
, respectively. When value converges to pattern specified in stage I, the second layer weights have almost no change.
But after stage I, the sign of gradient of second layer become fixed and aligned with its initialized sign, so that it will have no effect on other parameters.

F.4
Extension to Multi-head Attention

In this section, we discuss how to extend our main theoretical results, which focus on a single-head attention layer, to a multi-head attention layer. We note that our theoretical results can be easily extended to multi-head attention.

For a multi-head attention layer, let the parameters be

\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{Q,h},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{K,h},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{V,j,h}\right\}_{h=1}^{H}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{Q,h},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{K,h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}\times d}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{V,j,h}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{v}\times d}
for
j\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}
and
h\in[H]
.
Here, the
H
is the number of attention head, assumed to be a fixed constant.
Then, the network can be written as
f(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}):=F_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})-F_{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})
,
where
F_{1}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})
and
F_{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})
are defined as:

\displaystyle F_{j}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{h=1}^{H}F_{j,h}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}),

\displaystyle F_{j,h}(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{m_{v}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\textbf{1}^{\top}_{m_{v}}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{V,j,h}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}\text{softmax}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{K,h}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{Q,h}\boldsymbol{\mathbf{x}}^{(l)}\right).

Gradients in Multi-Head Attention.

Regarding the gradients, the partial derivatives of the single-head model outputs with respect to the parameters, i.e.,

\frac{\partial F_{j,h}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{Q,h}}
,
\frac{\partial F_{j,h}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{K,h}}
,
\frac{\partial F_{j,h}}{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}_{V,h}}
, remain unchanged.
However, the gradient of the loss with respect to the single-head model outputs, i.e.,

\frac{\partial\ell}{\partial F_{j,h}}
, does change.
This change, however, is linear and straightforward to analyze.
Intuitively, the model outputs increase approximately
H
times, causing the magnitude of the loss derivatives
\ell^{\prime}
to decrease accordingly.

Formally, our theory shows that in single-head attention, the loss derivative are close to initialization up to
t=4T_{4}^{-}
, where
4T_{4}^{-}
is the time when the sign alignment of negative query noise completes. Specifically, for all
i\in[n]
,
t\leq 4T_{4}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle\ell_{i}^{\prime(t)}:=\frac{\partial\ell}{\partial f(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(t)},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}_{i})}=1/2\pm o(1),

This implies
f(\boldsymbol{\mathbf{W}}^{(4T_{4}^{-})},\boldsymbol{\mathbf{X}}_{i})=o(1)
. Then, the
H
-fold increase in the multi-head model outputs does not alter this result, so the effect of changes in
\frac{\partial\ell}{\partial F_{j,h}}
can be neglected.

Consequently, the behavior of signals and noise still follows the four-stage dynamics in the single-head case, with the dynamics of all attention heads being the same.

Experiments.

In Figure
13
, we plot the full dynamics of our simplified transformer but with 4 attention heads. We can see that the dynamics of query noise, key noise, query signals, and key signals are identicalto those in the single-head model (Figure
18
). Additionally, the dynamics of the softmax outputs in each head are consistent.
These empirical evidences further support that our theory holds in the multi-head attention models.

Appendix G
More Detailed Explanations for the Four-Stage Dynamics

Figure 18:

The full training dynamics of two-layer transformers with SignGD.
This figure shows the dynamics of value, query, and key signals and noise, as well as softmax outputs and loss across all iterations.

(a)
Dynamics of value noise, and value signals.

(b)
Dynamics of query noise, key noise, query signals, and key signals.

(c)
Dynamics of softmax outputs.

(d)
Training and test loss curve.
Omitted notations are the same as Fig.
1
.

Figure 19:

Timeline of the four-stage training dynamics.

We mark all key points, including
T_{1}
,
T_{2}^{\text{SGN}}
,
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
,
T_{4}^{-}
,
T_{4}
, along with their corresponding key behaviors in the dynamics:

\raisebox{-.9pt}{1}⃝
: Mean value noise increases rapidly, becomes positive, and grows linearly with
t
.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{2}⃝
: Mean value signals remain stuck at their initialization.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{3}⃝
: Query and key noise are stuck at initialization.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{4}⃝
: Query and key signals are stuck at initialization.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{5}⃝
: Noise-signal softmax outputs are stuck at initialization.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{6}⃝
: Mean value noise continuously increases.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{7}⃝
: Mean value signals are negligible compared to mean value noise.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{8}⃝
: Query noise and key noise align their sign with each other.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{9}⃝
: Sign alignment completes, query noise and key noise continuously increase, and grow linearly with
t
.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{10}}⃝
: Query signals leave the initialization, align their sign with the majority of query noise, and grow linearly with
t
.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{11}}⃝
: Key signals leave the initialization, align their sign with the minority of query noise, and grow linearly with
t
.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{12}}⃝
: Query signals and key signals continuously increase.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{13}}⃝
: Noise-signal softmax outputs decrease exponentially.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{14}}⃝
:
Positive key noise continuously increases.
Negative key noise flip the update direction, and align with query signals.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{15}}⃝
:
Noise-signal softmax outputs reach
o(1)
and remain sufficiently small.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{16}}⃝
:
Positive query noise continuously increase;
Negative query noise flips update direction and aligns with query signals.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{17}}⃝
:
Negative key noise completes sign alignment, and all key noise continuously increases.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{18}}⃝
:
Negative query noise completes sign alignment, and all query noise continuously increases.

\raisebox{-.9pt}{\scriptsize{19}}⃝
:
All signals and noise continuously increase, and the loss decreases exponentially.
Note that by "increase", we refer to an increase in magnitude.

Appendix H
Discussion on Differences
from Real-World Setup

We would like to discuss the following key differences between our theoretical setups and the transformers used in real-world applications:

The gap in the data noise structure.

•

The data model considered in our study is simplified — we assume Gaussian noise that is i.i.d. across data samples and independent of the true features. All these simplifications inevitably create a gap from real-world datasets. Additionally, our data settings are motivated by image datasets. For language data, since language tokens have more dense semantic information, and since the noise must be discrete, the noise in language data could be more structured compared to the image data.

•

These differences are acknowledged in the Limitation section of our initial manuscript, and make it difficult to compare the noise in our data model and real-world language or image datasets. However, we would like to highlight again that even in this simplified data model, significant challenges present in the theoretical analysis.

The gap in tasks regarding optimization.

•

The sensitivity of SignGD to noise, as demonstrated in our findings, is relative to GD and is observed in a task where both optimizers achieve perfect convergence. In contrast, real-world language and vision tasks using transformers often reveal that GD struggles to optimize effectively, with a significant training loss gap compared to Adam
(
Zhang et al., 2024c
)
.

•

This highlights a gap between our task and real-world tasks in terms of optimization. This gap in tasks regarding optimization may also affect the generalization performance and robustness of the optimization methods. Additionally, as GD cannot optimize effectively in real-world transformers, it is difficult to fairly compare the generalization properties of GD and SignGD. Understanding why GD fails to optimize transformers on real-world tasks should be an important prerequisite.

•

However, we would like to emphasize that, although our task optimizes easily, our work provides a clear example where SignGD, and by extension Adam, fails in learning transformers, and hence is not always more effective than GD.

•

The reason behind this gap: This gap in the optimization may stem from factors such as simplified data structures and models in our setups. However, since our experiments on two-layer and three-layer transformers demonstrate consistent results regarding training dynamics (See Appendix B.6), we suspect this gap is more attributable to differences in data.

How to define robustness in the real-world transformers, particularly language models.

The concept of robustness in real-world language models requires careful consideration. In practice, when training transformers on language tasks with Adam, training instability and loss spikes are often observed, usually due to low-quality data batches
(
Chowdhery et al., 2023
)
. Given that transformer training typically involves billions of tokens, the generalization performance is often closely tied to the training performance. Thus, from this viewpoint, training instability and loss spikes can reasonably be viewed as a form of non-robustness. The message we aim to convey is: To fairly connect our theoretically motivated findings to real-world applications, it is crucial to carefully define what robustness means in practical transformers like language models.

In the above, we discuss the gap from the perspectives of data noise, optimization complexity, and the definition of robustness. We hope this discussion helps clarify the differences between our theoretical findings and real-world scenarios.
